ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e95	Honourable co-representatives! Former mayor Karen Sogn has passed away – 82 years old. Karen Sogn was born on 28 April 1931 in Oslo. After graduating artium and secretarial courses, she worked for a number of years in the family business, before moving to Lardal in Westfold in the early 1960s. Here she combined her role as a housewife with a broad commitment to the community. Among other things, Sogn had a job in the peasantry and the maid. In addition to being part-time libraries at the local public library, she quickly became involved in local politics. In 1967 she was elected to Lardal City Council for the Right. Here she sat for three periods and was vice-president from 1975 to 1977. At the 1973 elections, Karen Sogn was elected alternate to Stortinget, and in 1977 as permanent representative from Vestfold. In the course of time at Stortinget she was given the opportunity to work on matters which she personally was very concerned about. In the Church and Education Committee, she was involved in school issues and cultural and church issues. In the Agriculture Committee she also got to work with an industry she knew from the inside. Sogn was also a member of the Right Group Board and of the Storting delegation to the Council of Europe Parliamentary Assembly. After leaving Stortinget in 1985, Karen Sogn continued to work on a number of the issues she had been involved in in national politics. Among other things, she was Vice-Chairman of the Adult Education Council, a member of the Board of Directors of the Norwegian Agricultural Research Council and Vice-Chairman of the County Agriculture Board of Vestfold. She also went back into the management of the Lardal Right. The most important thing, however, was the work she made for church and church life in Lardal. She was for a number of years head of the parish council and head of the Church Council in Lardal. In the memory words of Lardal Right it says that «Karen Sogn was a real lady, with good sense». It's a characteristic of everyone who had the joy of working with Karen Sogn on the Stortinget, can join with full heart. She was highly respected and well liked by both representatives and employees. We remember Karen Sogn for her work on the Stortinget and brighten the peace of her memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e163	The budget adopted by the Stortinget today is, with some exceptions, a good budget for the knowledge sector. The budget follows in general what Mr Stoltenberg presented, a budget based on what has been our line for eight years with a red-green government: putting school ahead of tax cuts, focusing on knowledge and quality, and putting efforts into a good common school for all, where all children are seen and given the opportunity to realise their potential. The bet has given results. Although the PISA study of the 10th grade in 2012 did not give the promise that many had hoped for, we see that the students are getting more and more proficient and learning more and more. Several hours, several years' work, early work, clear learning goals and better systematic follow-up, together with learning-support tests, state aid for further training of teachers, better school building, homework and clear learning goals have produced results. Of course, we see it among the youngest students, those who have benefited most from the knowledge promise of the red-green government. We see that the ten-year-olds have a success equal to a full school year, and together with Finland now the best rains in the Nordic region in their year-olds. We're not on target, but we're on the right track, and that's why we give Norwegian teachers and students credit. Our task is to lay the groundwork for a good school to be even better. But we should never forget that it's the students, the teachers and the parents who do the main job. We must never forget to tell about the great and good job done in very many classrooms. The PISA study also shows progress in some areas. There is more serenity in classrooms, the possibility of concentrated learning is greater, a lot of students give credit for the cooperation between students and teachers, and Norwegian school is better able than the school in most other countries to balance for different social backgrounds among students. There has been broad agreement on many of the efforts in the Knowledge Lift. It started when I, as Minister of Education, dropped the Sögnen Committee in 2001. My successor extended the mandate and changed something on the composition. Luckily, the extra job that the sample was given to do in primary school from 10 to 9 years was never followed up by the Bondevik government. A whole school year less had hardly contributed to any level of knowledge. In 2004, the big message came, and with the 2006 budget, a red-green government was able to start implementing the Knowledge promise. However, there are some important differences, although perhaps in many ways now there is a broader consensus on the educational issues in school than for a long time. The most important thing is that you have to have a recognition that quality costs. In some of the budget debates here this fall, the Right Party and the Advance Party have spent a lot of time saying that there are other things than the size of the budget that count. And that's true. Organisation and the efficient use of money are important, but we also see that resources are crucial. It costs money to hire more teachers. It costs to have a school day that is attractive for young people to want as a career path. It'll cost you to expand your education by more hours. It costs to clean up school buildings and invest in ICT, or to provide adapted education to those who are struggling. In particular, it costs to provide continuing training to teachers. We national politicians therefore have a great responsibility, and it is to put children and young people's growth and education ahead of other good wishes that we must have – in front of the removal of tolls, the removal of inheritance fees or large tax cuts to those who have the most. Here the red-green parts and the right-hand and front-facing layers are separated. When the Right Party and the Advance Party use four billion more oil, we see that there is no knowledge that is put first. Overall, increased use of oil money goes to tax cuts. New efforts on knowledge are paid in full by cut in the same sector. Efforts to continue training for teachers are paid by cut in the cultural school hour. Professional bets are paid by cut in the students' food. And research is paid to drop the eleventh month of study funding. This is also evident in answers from the Ministry of Education, via finance, to the financial fraction of the Workers' Party. After first of all having calculated errors in the budgetary impact in 2015 – it is not only in the PISA study that mathematical knowledge may be a little disappointing – we finally got a corrected table that shows that the effects of the changes made in the supplementary proposition, at best in 2015 on the knowledge sector, are going to go to zero, probably a little less. So it's the reality of the change of pace that was announced in the press releases about the additional proposition. I think it's an unwise way to go. Torbjørn Røe Isaksen is the first Minister of Education – after what we have been able to figure out – who actually starts a reduction in the hours available to students. So you say yes, it's just the cultural school hour. Well – first, it’s important enough in itself, but if it was the device you disagreed with, the resources, the money, could have been used for other things. We should be open to a discussion about it. We know that so far many municipalities have spent this money on other things: increasing teacher's density, special education or other things. Now the money's gone. Then, of course, the possibility of using them also disappears. The question is, what will be the next year's bet? Now, once you have removed the cultural school lesson, once you have removed fruit and vegetables, what are the next offers to students who are going to pay for the further steps in the common goals we have, for example: to invest in continuing and continuing training of teachers? The red-green parties have a different strategy. Even without using one extra crown of oil, we're involved in many of the increases, without at the same time being involved in the cuts. We are involved in strengthening the professions, as we have been doing for many years in government. We're in on increased bets against bullying. We are involved in more money for the science centres, and we are united – the entire committee – in continuing to step up State aid for the further training of teachers. It was the red-green government that introduced such a solid contribution from the state to further training for teachers that the Workers' Party opted for that effort to be doubled during the current period. When the government in its supplementary proposition increases this, it's good, and we're in. But I have a piece of advice, I think, to all of us – not least to the Minister of Knowledge – and it is to recognize that it is neither the Right Party nor the Workers' Party, nor anyone else here in the Chamber, who has invented further training of teachers. That's what the school owners have been doing for years. What we have introduced is an ear-marked state grant for further training for teachers. At the end of the day, however, it is the school owner’s economy – it is the municipalities and county municipalities that employ teachers, who provide temporary workers and who facilitate further education – that will be crucial. That is why the fundamental choice is: how well do we finance the basic wall in the school sector, namely the municipalities and county councils that own the schools? Higher education and research have seen strong growth under the red-green government, a much stronger growth than the budget as a whole. That's right. We don't live by being the cheapest, we live by being the smartest. I think we have a lot to pick up on increasing the commercialization of the research we're doing, making sure that business-academia contacts get stronger, make the candidates get faster through, and make sure that we focus on the research environments that are world-class. I am happy to work closely with the sector in these areas in the years to come. The problem, however, is that the majority, together with the students, do not provide for the eleventh month of study. We have very good quotations from, among other things, The party's Tord Lien, who says that this is self-financing, because investing in study funding, increases the level of implementation, which is left with the money. It is a pity then, when Tord Lien and the Progress Party finally joined the government, that this is adding to the long list as enough a breach of promise from the Progress Party. We are also largely in agreement with the church budget. The reform of education, which was adopted by the Bondevik government, but which is once again implemented by the red-green government – we are the ones who have provided so to speak every single penny of the budgets we have submitted – is now on target. And then we might have slightly different opinions about what this goal is, but it's a huge bet. Finally, let me thank the Committee on Good Cooperation and the Committee Secretary for an excellent work. I look forward to a good debate on an important budget.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e277	When I was a kid, I was going to be a teacher. For me, it was a dream for many years, and through the studies, I acquired full teaching skills in real-life higher education. To me, there were coincidences that didn't make me a teacher, but it's no coincidence that I'm now committed to strengthening the school by just investing in the teaching profession. It is also not accidental that I get involved in the Right. Right went to the choice of realising the knowledge society, and in this budget we see a clear turn in the right direction for increased investment in knowledge. School is one of the most important institutions in society, and a good teacher is the most important single factor and one that matters most to the quality of school. The red-green government did not regain the confidence of the voters in the fall’s growing choice. It may have fallen red-green politicians heavy on the chest. One of the reasons for the elections may have been that the voters no longer had confidence in the government’s knowledge policy. Eight years of red-green governance leave behind measures such as proposals for the legalisation of cultural school hours, centralized child-care facilities and earmarked funds for fruit and vegetables. The right priority is to use the forces of targeted action for teachers. The knowledge gap, which was rolled out in 2006, gave a new one at school, improved somewhat, but the efficiency over time has been too poor. The recent results of PISA 2012 show that Norway has major challenges, especially in math and real science. It is particularly worrying that the results in mathematics have been reduced and that the location is worse than in 2009. Therefore, it is good that the government will now turn the school through a large-scale effort on the most important thing for learning, namely the teacher. The government will create new career paths for Norwegian teachers, so that the best teachers remain in the classroom. We want to increase the performance and strengthen teacher training, so that the best students apply to this profession. In the 2014 state budget, we give special priority to continuing education in mathematics and natural sciences. It's good when we know that we have a problem with the real subjects. The government’s draft budget for 2014 provides students with more annual support of 3.65 pst., or NOK 3 450. The government will bet on the students, they are important for the future, and they can look forward to better times with a blue government. The last time students had a real increase in student support was in 2002, where aid increased by NOK 10 500. Eight years in the Ministry of Knowledge has been no boost to research policy, and although the Workers' Party boasts of its own efforts, those who have been involved in research policy know that it is necessary to invest more targetedly and far heavier than what we have seen in recent years. Public consumption in general has grown enormously, and only two out of ten oil crowns have been used for what was the premise when the rule of action was established, namely investment in the future – investment in infrastructure, research and development and growth-promoting taxes. Many countries invest more than twice as much annually as Norway in research as a share of GDP, and have world-class educational institutions that train an annual surplus of highly educated relevant labour. The government's increase in research investment, increased freedom for institutions through increased basic appropriations and a binding long-term policy is a major step in the right direction. For me, it is also important to stress that the government believes that freedom of religion and belief is a fundamental human right and a prerequisite for a free society. The anchoring of the government will lie in the Christian and humanistic cultural heritage. The government emphasises that the Norwegian Church is of great importance to many Nordics, and will pursue a policy that ensures that the Church maintains the status of a people's church for all. I would particularly like to highlight increased appropriations for the pastoral service and the Seaman's Church. Finally, I would like to say a few words about Oslo. At the latest in the evening post today, we can read that the Oslo School is doing the best in the country. In Oslo, for many years, we have been working hard on monitoring and transparency on how the school performs. We have put in place targeted actions for better learning for all. In Oslo, fewer students belong to them with the lowest professional level than on a national basis, and the drop-out is lower than in the rest of the country. In addition, we know that children with low-skilled parents do better in Oslo than in the rest of the country. It shows that the Higher Education Policy, as practiced in Oslo, not only creates the best results, but also contributes to the creation of social mobility. A clear and clear prioritisation of knowledge in the 2014 budget shows that the government is serious when it says that the knowledge nation is now to be realised. Concrete growth in student funding — in addition to enhanced teacher training — and increased investment in research and innovation are good news for all those who are committed to strengthening our competitiveness in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e374	The Committee has had a short period of time in its budgetary work, and I would first like to thank you for good cooperation, which has enabled us to achieve good budgetary objectives. The right-wing Party government is betting on school and education, thus facilitating the development and growth of the welfare society of today and the future. "Education is the cheap defense of nations," said Edmund Burke – and with this government's offensive commitment to education, we are building a good defence for future generations in Norway. Norway is a high-cost country and in a global context, we cannot compete on price, but on quality and smart ways to work. The government aims to create a robust and diverse business and to make the Norwegian economy less oil dependent. Human capital is Norway's largest resource and accounts for about half of it. 80 pst. of our national wealth. We need to exploit this resource even better and put human capital into more valuable innovation. Bill Gates has said: I believe in innovation, and the way you get innovation is by investing in research and learning basic knowledge. This government is doing just that; it is clearly giving priority to basic knowledge and real science, and research is increasing by nearly half a billion dollars in relation to the budget of the Stoltenberg government. In order for us to make the best possible use of our greatest wealth, we need a good education offer at all levels – from kindergarten and primary school to adult education. Norway, as a knowledge society, is fully dependent on students from primary school to acquire knowledge to master the challenges they face in vocational education, study and working life. Last week the PISA study was presented. There we keep some reading skills, but unfortunately the students are making things worse in math and natural sciences, and we are thus at the level of what was called the PISA-shock year. This is not good enough. I want to say it so strongly that the value creation that we depend on stands and falls on real science. We're taking the PlSA results seriously. What PISA measures is the level of knowledge in mathematics, science and reading, and Norwegian students score poorly. Norwegian students are roughly on the OECD average in science and mathematics, and scores lower in mathematics now than ten years ago. These results are not proportionate to the use of resources. The evening post recently wrote on the front page: ‘After 10 years, 66 per cent more expensive school and 43 measures, the results of PlSA remain medium-sized.’ This underlines the need for a comprehensive real-time response and must be met in an offensive manner. We must have the will to put the most important thing first – that is what is to be given priority. For this government, real science is fortunately a priority area, which clearly bears the mark of the budget. The Progress Party has great ambitions on behalf of the students and is proud that the government parties, together with the Christian People's Party and the Left, promote a budget that contains a lot of concrete proposals that will make the school better and that are fully funded. Aristotle said that the foremost characteristic of having real knowledge is the ability to learn away. It is important for this government to facilitate the real knowledge of teachers. In the government’s teacher’s vow, continuing training is increased by 300 million. kr, which will allow 750 more teachers to improve their skills than the budget of the Stoltenberg government provided. Several have pointed out that many teachers who teach math and science do not have the necessary professional immersion in the subjects. That is why we give priority to continuing training in these subjects. Real science bets take place on a broad front. We are pleased that the government is clear, offensive and practical in its efforts to bring real science up to a level that the world's richest country can be familiar with: continuing education will pay particular attention to teachers in mathematics and the natural sciences; the good teachers will be even better; an offensive real science strategy will be developed; the national centres in real science will receive increased funding and we will receive a strengthening of science centres. The virtual mathematics school and the ability to take higher education courses will also be central. Closer contact with industry will be developed through the National Forum for Real Science, and we will receive a strengthening of professional requirements for teachers in school from 2014. In addition, there will be an increase in the number of doctoral degrees in real science and a notable increase in business-related research. Among a lot of good measures to strengthen the school and the teachers, I would also like to mention the commitment to the mentoring system for newly educated teachers. Today, in fact, one of three newly educated teachers quit the profession, and a better mentality could give them a better follow-up and damper practice as many newly educated teachers experience. Norway needs more competent professionals in different professions. We know that many drop out of school because of lack of apprenticeship and because the theory is perceived as too little professionally relevant. In order to improve education and to get more people to complete the government's vocational training: improving continuing education and training for teachers in secondary school, professional correcting the theory subjects, increasing apprenticeship grants and an incentive grant of DKK 50000 for new enterprises taking in apprentices. We are also very pleased that the government is providing additional grants to enterprises that include apprentices with special needs. The government will expand the practical letter-of-mortgage scheme and increase the appropriation by 10 million. kr. These are good measures, which will both prevent non-response and ensure more areas of learning. I would also like to stress the need for holistic thinking in school policy, and it is an ongoing approach in the government's budget: real-time education, teacher mobility and other quality promotions, flexible exchange schemes, better apprenticeship arrangements, increased dialogue and cooperation with business and practice-setting – all of this contributes to increasing quality in school. We will get less disqualification, more qualified students who are motivated to become skilled professionals, academics and researchers. This is reflected in the government’s increased focus on research and higher education and in the specific focus on engineering and real-life studies, innovation and industrial research. Innovation is a prerequisite for future value-creating activities. Norway’s value-generating industries are competence-based, and this will be even stronger in the future. Oil and gas, renewable energy, maritime/offshore and marine, finance and ICT are value drivers to safeguard the welfare society of the future. These are different industries, but everyone has one thing in common – they're based on real science. This government prioritizes: The government proposes 50 million. for the modernisation of the equipment park in engineering training. It is necessary in order to ensure high-quality and relevant engineering training for employment and business. The government grants money for teachers' vows instead of cultural school hours in the school leisure system. The government spends half a billion more on research than the Stoltenberg government. The government has clear leadership in its allocations and rates in the real subjects. This shows the will and ability of a policy for sustainability and value creation. The red-green Kuf budget contains only good wishes – and no concrete amendments – and the reason for this may be that they are satisfied with the budget submitted. At least the Progress Party thinks that there are a lot of good reasons to be! I see that my speaking time runs out, so I have to come back to the church budget in a separate post.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e500	A week and a half ago, the results of the PISA study were published, and some people were somewhat disappointed. I believe that raising the level of competence among teachers is one of the tools that need to be done, and I believe that the teachers' commitment to which it is now being put up, with a systematic commitment to the continuing training of teachers, is a step in the right direction. But we also have to help the teachers succeed. The Christian People's Party has long wanted to make the mentoring system compulsory for newly educated teachers. Then you get help and guidance early in your career to find out what works and what does not work in your class. I believe that competence in just class management and the dissemination of knowledge is as important as the sound expertise of the teachers. I also think it's a little about attitudes among students and parents. We've seen it materially very well in this country. The few need to wear for their daily bread. We might quickly get a sense that things are going to work out anyway. Then we all have a responsibility to cultivate motivation and good attitudes in our children by speaking positively about learning, school and teachers. But even though many people were a little disappointed with the PISA results, it's important that we can keep our heads cold. The solution is not a comprehensive commitment to more testing, more requirements and more reporting. It can quickly do more damage than good, and we can risk destroying what is actually positive in the PISA study, because the picture is not just black. There are also points of light. Students report less trouble than before, better learning environment and better interaction between teacher and student. It's great, and a good learning environment is crucial for students to be able to perform well. But it is essential, above all, that our children should have a safe and healthy everyday life at school. The PISA study also does not provide a full picture of the school and the challenges we face. We must not just focus on the somewhat weak math results. We know that more than 40,000 children are bullied regularly. For many, it means a day-to-day with discomfort and insecurity. For some it means a broken childhood. In the period 2001–2005, bullying was documented to be reduced by 30 pst. At that time, the country's prime minister lifted the fight against bullying as an important asset. In recent years, this work has not been given the same obvious priority, and now bullying has again become a major problem. The Stoltenberg government put down correctly, just before the elections, a sample to investigate measures to improve the school environment and combat bullying, and I look forward to this investigation being presented in June 2015. Now I hope that the country's new Prime Minister and Minister of Knowledge will once again raise this issue. It's about the dignity and quality of life of our children. I am pleased that the Christian People's Party (Christian People's Party) in the negotiations with the government parties and the Left was able to make 10 million. kr to a pot for which schools, municipalities and county councils can apply for support from concrete measures to improve the learning environment. This is a good opportunity to test out which actions work, and what we can continue to do. This could be done, for example, by: be an opportunity to test out a mob representative scheme in one or more counties. Another major challenge in the Norwegian school is the great dropout. Too many people do not complete secondary education, and too many do not complete higher education. I'd like to point out in particular at the colleges, which is a good alternative for many. To get more students to complete their education, we also need to make it easier to be a full-time student. It is a good investment in knowledge and competence. Schools are about more than just knowledge. It's not just education, it's education. It's education. It's about developing as a human being. Some schools differ slightly from other schools, and then I think about the high schools. This is a valuable, but often a little underestimated, supplement to the other educational provision. Many people say that the year of high school is the year that has helped most to shape them as human beings. This is going to be a folk high school that's going to give an offer to lonely young mothers, and I'm sure this very high school will mean way too many. It's the public school that makes up the support bar in the Norwegian school system, and that's how I think it must be in the future as well. However, it is important to provide private schools that provide a real alternative to public schools, either because they offer alternative pedagogics, or because they provide an alternative based on faith. Such schools are necessary to ensure parents’ right to choose a different type of school for their children from that offered by the public, and this is a right enshrined in international conventions. There has to be maximum economic equality between public and private schools, and I am therefore pleased that the Christian People's Party has been able to put in place a capital grant for these 10 million free schools. kr. This is a beginning, and the Christian People's Party will work to raise this amount in future budgets. Private actors also represent an important complement in higher education, and private universities contribute not least to increasing the breadth and diversity of the study offer. In higher education too, there is a need for a greater level of non-discrimination between private and public actors. We have to make sure that the private universities get funding for their infrastructure so that they don't push the bill over to the students. The private study offer should not only be an offer for students with a good economy. We also need to ensure that private actors get their share of new study places and scholarship positions. I am therefore pleased that the majority of the Committee agreed on an opinion that the approximately 100 new scholarship positions put in place after the negotiations should be allocated to both private and public institutions. I will end by pointing out that for several years the Christian People's Party has called for a stronger commitment to knowledge, education and research. I think we all agree that this is necessary to ensure the country's well-being in the coming years. The budget now adopted is an important step in the right direction.

But not everyone is willing to prioritize the teacher as much as what we in the left have done. In our alternative state budget, we added 550 million. kr more than the red-green for teacher empowerment measures. Of course, we are pleased that one in the budget that will be adopted has strengthened this area, but we are concerned that empowerment is something that all teachers have both the right and the duty to take, not just those who teach mathematics and science. Kids are whole people, who have the benefit and joy of mastering in every subject. The formal competence of the teacher is also important for the teaching of subjects other than mathematics and natural sciences. The left will therefore work to ensure that subsequent increases in the number of continuing training places are not only reserved for these two subjects. The left is also occupied with the approx. 10 000 non-qualified teachers working in Norwegian school today. These are people who have chosen to work at school because they like to work with children and young people, and because they have a lot to contribute here. Then we have to have a plan for how we can best organise that this group, which is very complex, gets the skills that are necessary. In the left, we are very pleased with the point in the budgetary agreement with the government parties, where it appears that the government has to work out a modified scheme for teaching aid, so that today's statutory system can be terminated and the individual school or school owner can organise the homework according to their needs. We are looking forward to this investigation being presented in connection with next year's government budget. Wherever I meet school leaders and teachers, the feedback is that today's system is too rigid, and that they themselves want to dispose of these funds to provide homework to those who need it the most. They themselves know how to organise this in the best possible way. We are also pleased with the government's signals that it will be easier to make your career in the classroom. This must be an essential part of the teacher's lift. We made proposals on this in the previous period. We meet the further work on this. In our alternative state budget, we also strengthened the study federations by 5 million. kr. After a re-priority in the committee, this pot was strengthened by 1 million. kr within the framework. By strengthening the study associations, we are facilitating people to acquire skills throughout their lives in different ways outside the traditional education system. As we in the Left see it, learning something new is a value both for individuals and for society, regardless of whether it follows the study score. Here we differ a little from the Right Party and the Advance Party, which, through the comment in the committee's position, places great emphasis on the study points. Let me be quite clear that the left is for eleven months' study support. We're going to keep working for this. The red-green government was also for eleven months' study support, but this was not added until they had lost the election and had not had to take the bill. Nevertheless, we are pleased that we have included the government parties in a budget agreement stating that the government is to investigate the student's living conditions, with a view to realising the full-time student. The agreement clearly states that such an investigation should involve a plan to increase the support for the study and the length of the study aid. As we in the left understand this, that means that we're going to get a study grant beyond the ten months it is today, and that therefore we're going to move towards the eleventh month. There are also a number of other things that are important to improve student welfare. In particular, the construction of student housing is important in this context. The left added to its alternative budget an increase of 700 new student homes, a total of 2,000 student homes, which we also promised in our programming. The report ‘Questionnaire for and offer of scholarship positions in Norway by 2020’ estimates the need for up to 400 new scholarship positions annually in the period up to 2020. To fill this need, it's time for a power cap. The left added 500 new scholarship positions to its alternative budget. In the budget agreement with the government, we had the advantage of a strengthening of about 100 scholarship positions. It's a step in the right direction, but it's something we have to bet on in the future, and we have clear expectations for the government that there will be a more offensive bet on scholarship positions in the future. We are also pleased that we received a majority for a comment in the Committee where it is clear that the new universities should be given priority in the award of the new scholarships. If you want to bet on research and higher education, you don't just have more scholarship positions. The left has also wanted to increase the basic compensation of universities and colleges, we have added 2,000 more places of study, and we have contributed funds to 100 new postdoc jobs in our alternative state budget. So a little about tuition. I think a lot of people were surprised when the government added a point about the tuition fee for students outside the EEA. This is contrary to long tradition in Norway that all education should be free of charge for all, and many are afraid that such a tuition fee will in the long term lead to a study fee for all. I am therefore pleased that the bulk of the population has so clearly said no to the tuition fee. The Church and other religious and life-view societies are important social institutions, and the Left believes that we should pursue a holistic and active belief and life-view policy. The left is therefore satisfied with the budgetary agreement with the government parties where both religious training and ICT in the Church were strengthened. As far as church buildings are concerned, these have an important role to play in many communities. In several places, church buildings are also cultural memories that we have to take care of. In the alternative budget of the Left, we found room for a grant to start the refurbishment of the Stavanger cathedral. This is an important work, and I hope there is something the Minister of Culture will look at in the coming time. I am also pleased that in the committee we found room for a small allocation to the Church's resource centre against violence and sexual abuse. There is an important work done here.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e2050	Let me also take this opportunity first to thank the chairman of the committee for the well-executed management of the committee through the budgetary procedure. Then I want to start something back in time. A little over a hundred years ago, Norway was one of the poorest countries in Europe. The footprint was then tamed, electrons were produced and values created. Concession laws were designed, the interests of the nation and the people were cared for by forward-looking politicians. Eternal values were to belong to the many and not to the few. During the industrial journey, the people were lifted out of poverty, and opportunities for each were increased. By transporting electrons around the country, people, businesses and industry received electricity, perhaps the most important input of our time in a well-functioning society. Our story is about building up community values and strengthening people's opportunities. From state power stations we established Statnett and Statkraft, and State power is now the largest on renewable energy in Europe. In 1965, for the first time, the Norwegian authorities granted concessions for the search for petroleum in the North Sea. In 1969 results were seen, and Ekofisk was the first field to be opened, in 1971, of the time Prime Minister Trygve Bratteli. In 1972, following a unanimous decision in the Stortinget Statoil, which is today the world's largest offshore operator, has delivered significant values to the community through its 40-year history and has activity in 34 countries and over 23 000 employees. When Norway's oil and gas history started, we had to import almost everything we needed. Today, the supplier sector is our second largest transport industry, after oil and gas. It is knowledge from Norway and Norwegian suppliers that currently has 50 pst. of the world subsea drilling and 70 pst. of the world market in seismics. In 1972, Trygve Bratteli was the first prime minister in the world to establish its own Ministry of Environmental Protection. Olav Gärevoll became our first environmental protection minister. We have also, through our close history, placed ourselves in the forefront of tackling major global challenges such as the environment and climate. Gro Harlem Brundtland, with the Brundtland Commission, has set its clear mark on the fact that today’s people must not consume at the expense of future generations. Jens Stoltenberg's participation in The Global Commission on the Economy and Climate is also a recognition of a great commitment over a long period of time. History has not shaped itself, it has been created through political visions and discussions and often brings strong contrasts – far from unanimous. Yes, even in our very near history, these historical lines have been discussed at a great distance between the political parties in Stortinget. The political majority in the last eight years have developed Norway's opportunities and sought to preserve the generations of the future in a perspective based on the success factors of our history. Enormous values and great opportunities have been created, not through the budget we are now discussing, but through the history of creating a framework for the budget we are dealing with today. What did we see after a few weeks of blue-blue policy orientation? What clarifications were included in the budget? The blue-blue value orientation was alarming. The major challenges of our time – climate and poverty – were the areas that were most deprived, for the benefit of those who have the most at home here. Selfishness, or glorification of its own, is an appropriate expression of such a political priority. Combating poverty in the world was down-priority through significant cuts in aid, reducing climate action vigorously. It was a value orientation we witnessed. The cut in the rainforest bet was perhaps the most serious, but cut in the bet at the technology center in Mongstad, cut in Gassnova, cut in Transnova and cut in the stake in renewable energy in developing countries, and this is a significant weakening of climate action from a government that says they will strengthen climate reconciliation. There is no evidence of increased climate action in the budget. So it's good that the Christian People's Party and the Left get to turn on some of this in the budget deal. But that does not give much credit – it is the parties that have established the government and that is the parliamentary basis. There is reason to be concerned about the signals coming in the budget from the blue-blue government. In yesterday's debate, Minister for Industry, Monica Mæland said that: ‘parts of competitive activities face high costs. Therefore, in the 2014 budget, the government proposes to strengthen the competitiveness of industry in both the short and long term.» On the other hand, she added that the government ‘contributes to stable framework conditions and an active industrial policy promoting Norwegian value creation both for 2014 and in the coming years’. I am most curious about this statement and this analysis. After the presentation of the blue-blue budget, the Commission expressed its opinion in particular on: Hydro-director Halvor Molland himself like this in the Haugesunds Avis on 14 November: ‘These sudden and unexpected changes in the framework conditions make planning for the future even more demanding. Large, long-term investments in Norway — as we have seen in recent years — are becoming more difficult to imagine. It was said after the blue-blue government had virtually ripped the floor out of the CO2 compensation scheme for the industry without insight into the sensitivity of the industry. It was a proposal which naturally aroused strong reactions, and which clearly revealed that the blue-blue government is not near to understanding the very demanding situation of the industry. Every day, our industrial environments are working to attract investments that secure the future. It is a demanding job, and many countries are competing for future investment. Good framework conditions and predictability are key words. Uncertainty was created, and all signals stress that the uncertainty is not gone, it is still there. I therefore believe that the Stortinget must have as a general political principle and as a political guideline that Norwegian industry should be subject to good, competitive and predictable framework conditions. Part of this will, of course, be that the CO2 compensation scheme is maintained as a fully-fledged scheme during the contractual period until 2020. It is good that the government is now in favour of our original proposal. But the challenge for the government is nevertheless to convince the Norwegian industry that they will maintain the good framework conditions that came into place under the red-green government. It is therefore absolutely necessary that the government parties themselves take responsibility for removing the uncertainty they themselves have created for the Norwegian industry, by voting in favour of our proposal where we clearly ask that the government should ensure predictability for Norwegian industry by maintaining a fully-fledged CO2 compensation scheme in the contractual period up to 2020. Unfortunately, I do not think there is a common will to ensure this predictability for industry by providing a guarantee. The blue-blue government guarantee should not go to important industrial workplaces around the country. The guarantee given by the blue-blue is only about enriching those who have the most. The budget proposal from the blue-blue government parties was a reflection where we got an insight into the value orientation the government is going to have over the next four years, a blue-blue value orientation where I think we're going to see in the continuation that aid is also going to be reduced, a policy where climate action and less climate exposure are being characterised, and where tax cuts to the richest are prioritised over security for Norwegian industrial workplaces. It's unsolvable, it's old-fashioned, and it's unpredictable to conduct such a policy. This is a value orientation I like very badly, and that violates our historical success factors. I am not looking forward to the future with the background of the signals and the value orientation of the blue-blue government in its budget. At the end of the day, I would like to address the proposals in which the Labour Party is co-sponsored. I would also like to inform you that under the setting C, II, we would like to be allowed to vote against.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e2201	Norway has unique natural resources that need to be managed and processed for the benefit of society and future generations, whether we are talking about flora or fauna, footfalls or fossil energy. The Norwegian petroleum industry accounts for a quarter of gross domestic product — one third of the country’s income and half of the nation’s export income. It employs one tenth of the workforce and accounts for one third of the value creation. The importance of the petroleum industry for the Norwegian economy is evident. It is therefore positive that there is a broad consensus on the Stortinget on the road further for the Norwegian petroleum industry. At the same time, the size of the industry makes us more vulnerable to fluctuations in the oil price than ever before. The long-term transition to a low-emission society makes it necessary to have multiple thoughts in your head at the same time. It is therefore positive that the government has so clearly signalled an offensive policy to strengthen the framework conditions for business in mainland Norway, among others. by strengthening road and rail investment, research and development, environmental technologies and growth-promoting tax incentives, but also by strengthening and integrating climate policy into all policy areas. Norway can never become a low-cost country, but we can strengthen our competitiveness by developing and implementing climate-friendly technology. Then we have to play with business and the knowledge environments. Green thoughts must be able to be translated into black numbers in enterprises. An active and strong business that creates values and jobs is not a contradiction but a prerequisite for solving environmental problems. This applies not least in the energy sector. Norway has a significant potential to create values based on renewable energy. The Swedish-Norwegian market for electricity certificates helps to increase production by 26.4 TWh by 2020, but if the power is to have a value, it has to be applied. Then there are three purposes that are particularly important. Firstly, phase out fossil energy where possible and appropriate. The ban on fossil fuel oil firing from 2020 is a good start, but we will also see increased power needs in the transport sector and in the petroleum sector in the coming years. Secondly, the organisation of new environmentally friendly industrial production. Thirdly, play a greater role in the supply of power in our neighbouring European countries as a supplier of balancing power in markets with a lot of sun and wind power. The new inter-national links with Germany and the United Kingdom will be important to achieve this. It is not just new renewable energy that is needed to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Norway has technology, competence and capital to make a difference in many areas. Priority needs to be given to measures that can reduce emissions at home but also have an impact on the environment. Oil resources provide us with space for action to devote considerable resources to technology development, the conservation of rainforests, support for renewable energy and a faster transition of the transport sector. As a large petroleum producer, Norway has both a responsibility and a self-interest in developing a cost-effective technology for the capture and storage of CO2. Since 2009, the right has pointed out that there have been major challenges with the full-scale project in Mongstad and has clarified that costs need to be significantly reduced if realising is to be appropriate. We want the first full-scale CO2 capture and storage facility funded by Norway to provide encouragement and inspiration for other countries and actors – not to be left to fear and warning. If CO2 management is to be valued as an emission reduction measure, it must be possible to use it in the long term by more than the world's richest countries. The complexity of the CO2 management challenges suggests that it makes sense for the government to develop a new strategy for Norway’s work on this. Voluntary forest protection is a very important tool to meet our national and international commitments to halt biodiversity loss. The right has long called for increased appropriations for this purpose and is pleased that the allocation in 2014 allows for the collection of parts of the catchment area after the field was severely deprioritised in the first seven years of the red-green managed. The international forestry effort is also very important. The effort to reduce deforestation in the rainforest is an effective tool to reduce global greenhouse gas emissions, but it is also an important contribution to maintaining the biological tax base of the rainforest and the life base of very many people living in or out of the forest. This is why the Right and the other opposition parties at the time proposed – and passed – an international forest effort during the negotiations on the first climate agreement. It is very positive that the government has announced that the investment will continue until 2020. It ensures predictability for the recipient countries. So finally – let me say to the representative of Aasland's history: it was the opposition parties who, through two climate settlements, pushed the previous government to adopt a much more offensive attitude on climate issues. It's very interesting when the representative Aasland is now trying to represent it as if the Workers' Party was the driver, while the rest of us were the brake block. The truth is the exact opposite. I am pleased that the new government wants to pursue a more ambitious climate policy than the previous government did, because it is urgent to find a solution to the climate challenges.

The removal of the prism floor means an increased industrial effort of a total of 123 mills. kr. ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e2985	Climate change is not happening once in the future. Climate change is now happening. The cooperation between the Christian People's Party, the Left, the Advance Party and the Right makes Norway meet four green years. Compared to the 2013 budget, the government proposes to increase the general rate of the CO2 tax on mineral oil and gas by approx. NOK 100. The base tax on mineral oil is also increased. At the same time, we reduce taxes on labour and value added. It is a good package for both the environment, business and workers. The green shift is in progress. We are strengthening the climate agreement as we increase the appropriations for the railways. We reinforce the climate agreement when we notify a green tax commission. We reinforce the climate agreement when we are working on introducing a deduction for ENØC measures in households. Renewable production is to be increased and the procedure is to be reduced. We will also invest more in new climate-friendly technology and research. The government is now working on a new carbon capture and storage strategy, with the ambition of full-scale clean-up in 2020. In 2014, the government will also increase the investment in environmentally friendly vehicles, including: because the VAT on the rental of an electric car disappears. Carbon leakage is a growing problem. The most important response to this challenge is to provide good framework conditions and favourable tax rules for Norwegian industry. But special measures may also be necessary. The scheme of industrial CO2 compensation is such a special measure. The scheme runs from 2014 to 2020 and is a targeted tool for carbon leakage. I am satisfied with the device it has now received. The fact that it is located in the Ministry of Climate and Environment underlines the climate dimension of the scheme. I was recently at the climate negotiations in Warsaw, where a road map for the future was adopted. Uganda’s Finance Minister, Maria Kiwanuka, was central to funding. The way she communicated it, that the climate is now focused on all planning in her country, gave me inspiration. The basis for a new climate agreement must be drawn up in 2014 to provide opportunities for negotiations in Paris in 2015. Norway shall continue to play an active and constructive role internationally. Our main challenge is to reduce CO2 emissions, but increasing efforts to reduce emissions of short-lived climate drivers, such as methane and soot, can be crucial in the short term to meet the two-degree target. In addition, reducing deforestation will result in significant reductions in greenhouse gas emissions. The Warsaw Climate Summit adopted a complete code for forest conservation. It's a milestone and an important breakthrough. We're going to spend 2.8 billion. kr in order to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation in 2014 and the fact that the government will continue the initiative until 2020 contributes to predictability. Developing countries are now receiving a strong signal to continue their efforts to reduce deforestation. Climate and environmental issues are also local. We need to preserve forests in Norway. The allocation to the new forest protection system is increasing by 100 million. kr in 2014. It makes it possible to conclude agreements on voluntary protection with forest owners. Norway has a close relationship with the coast, sea and water, so we will give priority to clean-up activities in the polluted seabed and increase it by 4 million. kr. Rapid climate change and increasing activity in the north are increasing the need for more knowledge, and therefore we are proposing an increase of 43 million. kr for climate and environmental research. Norway is blessed with an oil fortune that gives us great opportunities. But oil wealth also gives us a great responsibility – a responsibility for creating a sustainable society. The low-emission society is not an airy vision. The green shift is now in progress. It's about grasping the opportunities now, and the budget we're about to adopt today helps more people do just that.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e3115	The first thing most of us do when we wake up in the morning is to check if the cell phone is charged during the night. Then we get up, turn on the light and the coffee machine. The few of us think a little more about what makes the cell phone charged, the light is switched on when we turn on the switch, or the coffee is taken. That the current comes from a place, that someone is working to make sure that we get the current where we live, we take for granted no matter where we live, whether we live near or far from where the current is produced. In a well-functioning society, security of energy supply is critical. We've all experienced losing power for a longer or shorter time, and that's only when we're reminded of how dependent we are on energy. It is important that we ensure efficient, secure, robust and environmentally friendly energy supply in Norway. Over the past eight years, there has been a positive development in the entire energy field, and this is what we see as the main continuation of the Right-wing Party Government: the introduction of electricity certificates, increased capacity in renewable energy concession treatment, a historical commitment to refurbishment and renewal of the electricity grid, and increased investment in environmentally friendly conversion of energy use and energy production, to mention something. In the case of electricity certificates, we are well on track in Norway. The government has warned to consider adjustments to the system of electricity certificates, but what does this imply? Should a include renewable power built before the entry into force of the electricity certificates scheme? And how will it lead to more renewable power? In particular, a government made up of market librarians alerts changes that will affect the market, without doing anything about it. It is important that the government soon clarify what kind of adjustments they will propose in the Electricity Certificate Scheme. Electronic certificates are one thing, but notifying the introduction of tax deductions for an increment measure in households without doing anything about it is even more serious. In general, Norway is supplied with renewable energy, and most of it comes from hydropower. The fact that we get our current from hydropower makes us particularly vulnerable in some periods. It is necessary to work continuously to increase the robustness of the power supply towards periods of reduced and unforeseen events. Our government provided inter-national connections to both Germany and Great Britain, and they are now in concession treatment. This is important in order to ensure security of supply, achieve our offensive climate targets and contribute to green value creation. Experience in recent years has shown that we have been dependent on connections abroad to secure ourselves in periods of low rainfall and low temperatures. Several connections will strengthen the power exchange and mitigate fluctuations in power prices throughout the year. We will continue to build socially profitable cables. In the Sundvolden Declaration, the Right Party Government announces that they will amend the Energy Act so that actors other than Statnet will be able to own and operate foreign tables. We in the Workers' Party are opposed to such a change. We fear that this will mean that the state will have reduced control of network investments and important capacity and market conditions, and that this will result in increased vulnerability in the Norwegian market. The construction of foreign capacities will have implications for the national power grid. Therefore, private operators should not be allowed to do so. The fact that the government parties have received support from the Christian People's Party and the Left for continuing to cut off the contribution to the balancing of network tariffs is surprising, and it testifies to a large majority with low ambitions for the districts. Cuts in the balancing of network tariffs increase household, agricultural and, in particular, business expenditure. In particular, it affects those parts of the country with the absolute highest costs of transmission and distribution of electricity, due to challenging natural conditions and the structure of food and housing. When one sees this budget as a whole and in particular the proposal to remove regional development funds and the proposal to change the CO2 compensation scheme, which would have affected industry if it had obtained a majority, it is difficult to look at the budget as particularly district friendly. Keeping smaller network companies means a lot to security of supply for most people — in addition to providing local jobs and knowledge. If the government aims to reduce the number of network companies, it will start at the wrong end. The consequence will be, in the first place, not fewer network companies, but more expensive bills for the inhabitants of the districts.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e3146	In the case of renewable energy, we are part of a power market in the Nordic region. In addition, Europe is developing its new energy market. A great deal of new renewable energy is to be phased into the EU – in all significant sun and wind. Germany is about to reach 25 pst. renewable energy. The problem — to the extent that it is a problem — is that the energy has to be produced and used when there is wind and sun. This means that top loads are needed and, of course, other energy sources are needed to cover the rest of the need and to be reserve when there is no wind and no sun. As of today, the CO2 quota price is low. The United States has largely gone from coal to gas. Knowing that the gas price in Europe is higher than in the United States means something to the EU's competitiveness in energy-intensive production. According to Chief Economist Dr. Birol of the International Energy Agency, this bias is likely to continue until 2030. This means that coal in Europe is cheap, and we see a tendency for coal to be preferred to gas. In addition, the EU's renewable efforts will require the building of new networks and new structures. In addition, we are facing the introduction of a third EU electricity market package. With this as a hindrance, it is therefore good that the government has signalled that it will provide an energy message with an overall approach where one sees climate, energy and economic development as a whole. Although the backstop is somewhat unclear, renewable energy is and will become important, and the cheapest and most environmentally friendly use of power takes place through energy efficiency improvement measures. Making us consumers use less and only renewable energy is and becomes important. In this budget, the government has been committed to reducing our consumption by increasing efficiency through increased investment in research and development. Good conditions are also being worked on for an additional measure to be taken by households. The cooperationagreement between the Right, the Progressive Party, the Left and the Christian People’s Party states that a tax deduction should be introduced forinvestments in the home. This system shall be put in place as soon as possible. Due to the neglected development and maintenance of the electricity grid, there has to be a significant effort in the coming years. Almost whatever we do in a modern society, we depend on power and stable power supply. To improve this, it's signalled from Statnett that there will be an investment of between 50 and 70 billion. kr. In addition, there will be two investments in the regional networks. Furthermore, there is a need for reinforcement of the distribution system, including: through the introduction of advanced measurement and management systems in all households. Many of these investments need to be made due to security of supply. In addition, the phase-in of new renewable energy and the needs of the petroleum sector are in need of more power. The investment will be paid, and the network costs will have to rise. In this picture, it is important to safeguard the competitiveness of our industry. We do that by providing industry with a stable energy supply and good framework conditions, as the government puts into its budget. In addition, the aim is to ensure a good balance in energy supply by planning to expand inter-national connections, initially two new cables to Europe. What about renewable energy? The government contributes to increased production of hydropower, wind power, bioenergy and other renewable forms of energy. In other words, a major effort is being made to develop renewable energy in the country in the coming year. We believe that the future is renewable, and then we need to build renewable energy. If we're going to achieve our climate goals, we all agree that we need to expand. We have to expand the grid to deploy the power, we have to expand the industry that processes the power in the world's most environmentally friendly way. We are today in a regime with Sweden linked to green certificates. We've been working on expanding 13.5 TWh by 2020. Time is starting to get buttony. Major investments must have been adopted during 2014 and early 2015 in order to reach the certificate market. The scheme runs until 2020, and many investments will depend on their reaching this scheme. All investments to be made in the central and regional networks up to 2020 are significant, and we believe that in order to achieve this in a good and efficient way, we need a more rational network structure with fewer and more robust network companies – this in order to meet consumers' interests and needs. One of the measures that can be easily taken is to remove the manning requirements of the regulation of competence and replace it with general quality and safety requirements. We trust that those whom we have given the concession to operate our electricity grid also establish their operations in a safe and sound manner within their own HMS procedures that meet safety requirements. So finally some words about flood and escape. The government is increasing its investment in this area in the budget of the year. This is important. Here it is important to focus on ensuring existing construction and infrastructure and not least focus on future planning and new construction.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e3314	Petroleum industry is the largest and most important individual industry in Norway. It is characterised by large and long-term investments that generate huge revenues to the community and provides 250000 jobs across the country. Stable framework conditions are very important for this industry. It is therefore positive that the new government is continuing the petroleum policy of the red-green government with a focus on increased extraction, the development of findings and high exploration activity. However, the fourth pillar of petroleum policy, the opening of new areas, has been chosen to ignore. As late as May this year, today's government parties wrote in the Innst. 288 S on the permanent protection of the Lofoten: ‘The members of the Committee from the Party of Advances and the Right will underline the importance of keeping a predictable and high pace in opening new areas of petroleum activity’.» In addition, they say: ‘These members will initiate an impact assessment of oil and gas activity in Northland VI, Northland VII and Troms II.’; ‘... the importance of keeping a predictable and high pace’ did not prove to be greater than that this was already dropped in the experiments with the Left and Christian People’s Party. This is one of many areas where this government goes back to what the parties said before the election. In the government’s rhetoric about climate, it is said that they will strengthen climate reconciliation. In their first budget, they weaken it. In the proposal for XIX, the government proposed to abandon the ambition of the climate agreement to realise at least one full-scale CO2 capture facility by 2020, and they would replace it with an ambition to realise a demonstration facility. I am pleased that an overall committee rejects this and promotes the red-green government's proposal on this point. On the allocation side, the government cut Gassnova's budget by 10 million. kr on the grounds that the planning of full scale capture and storage is completed. But this was, of course, also provided for in the budget of the red-green government. And then we cut 100 million. kr in loans to TCM. This reduces the potential for better use of existing infrastructure. Similarly, increased testing opportunities are reduced and the TCM becomes less attractive to technology suppliers and industrial partners. Thus, the right-hand Party Government has put all its brakes on the development of CO2 capture and storage technologies. We all know that this is the most important single technology available to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. We also know that there is an urgent need to achieve emissions reductions as monsaries in order to achieve the goal of reducing global temperature increase to two degrees over pre-industrial time. The government has taken time out in technology development to create a new strategy. They don't say anything about when this strategy is going to be put in place, and they are cutting dramatically in the budgets for technology development. This leads to confusion of competence and delays in work. Global warming has not taken time out and government's climate policy ambitions appear weak. The CO2 compensation scheme is an important element of an effective climate policy. We ensure that the competitiveness of our hydropower-based process industry is not weakened as a result of an effective climate policy. This scheme was presented as crucial for the industrial companies that I visited in Hardanger during the election campaign. Good and stable framework conditions provided the basis for the plans for billions of investments at Tizir in Tyssedal, and they provided the basis for the hope of profitable operations at Søral in Husnes. The proposal of the Right-wing Party Government does not receive a majority, and it is the proposal of the Red-Green Government that is being adopted here today. That's good. Nevertheless, the industry is left with uncertainty. Will the CO2 compensation scheme suffer the same fate as the net pay scheme for seafarers? It had to be fought through in the budget procedure of the Great Britain twice a year under the previous bourgeois government. This does not create stability, it does not lay the foundation for investment, it does not become competitive. At Søral in Kvinnherad, 60 men had to go to our house because of the market situation. The 230 remaining employees deserve predictable framework conditions when they fight to secure the local community's cornerstone. Tizir in Tyssedal needs predictable framework conditions to convert a production technology to double production, create 100 new jobs and reduce CO2 emissions by 90 pst. This requires investments in the billion class. This is why we invite the Stortinget to decide to ask the government to ensure predictability of a fully-fledged CO2 compensation scheme in the contractual period until 2020. It is sad to hear that the government and the Minister for the Environment are unable to give a clear answer to this. They give clear answers and clear ties to the government's other arrangements, but they will not guarantee this. That is why we must promote the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e3368	Norway's history as an oil and gas nation is in many ways an adventure. Until the 1970s, GDP per capita in Norway was below the average in Western Europe. After that time, Norway has experienced significantly faster and more stable growth than most other countries. While economic growth has increased and declined in most other industrialised countries, it has almost only risen and progressed in Norway. After Norway’s new future as an oil nation had become a reality, what was to become known as the ten oil bids were written in 1971. Already in this early phase of our new role as an oil and gas nation, we had fortunately witnessed politicians in the British Industry Committee who knew what kind of significant responsibility our gold discovery under the sea brought with them. The first oil bid says as follows: ‘The national control-command must be ensured for all activities on the Norwegian continental shelf.’ This is an important principle, which Norway has maintained since the beginning of the oil adventure. Through the establishment of the Oil Directorate and Statoil in 1972, Petoro and Gassco in 2001, as well as the Petroleum Control Agency in 2004, full national governance and control has been sought. The government is committed to preserving and strengthening the state's ability to ensure the highest possible value creation from our fossil resources. Therefore, the government is strengthening both OD and Petoro in this budget. In this way, the OD's ability to contribute to increased extraction will be increased and Petoro's ability to follow up the interests of the country, in particular those in existing fields, will be improved. Increased extraction and continuous investment are necessary in order to maintain an even level of activity over time. Fortunately, there is a broad consensus on the importance of, and the direction for, the Norwegian petroleum industry. Still the ten oil bids lie as a map for the road further. This is good, because predictability and long-termity are very important for this industry. The petroleum industry is the engine of the Norwegian economy. 250,000 people are directly or indirectly employed here. The country collects one third of its income from it — nearly 350 billion. kr for 2013. There is no doubt that Norway, as we know today, would look very different without the oil and gas industry. Some would argue that we need to phase out and shut down Norwegian petroleum industry in order to combat world climate change. I think climate change is something we have to take very seriously. Norway also has to do its part and has a responsibility. However, I do not agree that the closure of Norwegian oil and gas activities is the solution to the problem. Climate challenges need to be seen in a global perspective. The world is increasingly in need of more energy. More and more are being raised out of poverty, and more and more countries are experiencing economic growth. I believe our petroleum industry is an important contribution to solving the world's energy and climate challenges. As early as 1971, we were concerned about the environment in oil policy. The fourth oilman says: «The development of an oil industry must take place taking into account existing industrial activities and the protection of nature and the environment». We've been busy with this page. Norwegian continental shelf accounts for the world's most climate-friendly oil and gas production. The emissions per unit of oil and gas produced from Norwegian cap are significantly lower than from the petroleum provinces of other countries. This has not happened by itself. There is considerable willingness to convert and to develop new and more environmentally friendly technologies behind this. Norwegian gas production, precisely because of good technology, is significantly more environmentally friendly than gas production in many other parts of the world. Therefore, it is important that we increase our efforts on research so that we can continue to use our oil wealth to develop new technologies to help the world achieve its climate goals. In this budget, there are 36 million. kr more for petroleum research, it is a good start. Less access to oil and natural gas is likely to lead to increased use of coal in many countries. The IEA states that the energy mix will also have a significant element of fossil energy in the future in the green scenario. The question is first and foremost the proportion to be recovered from coal or from oil and natural gas. Norwegian petroleum activity is part of the solution. This does not mean that we should not continue to invest in renewable energy and other environmentally friendly solutions. This is not about either – or, but it is about both – and.
