ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e4134	The Kyoto Protocol was adopted at the Third Conference of the Parties to the Kyoto Convention in 1997. For the first time, a legally binding international agreement on climate change was adopted, with numerical emission obligations for those countries that were then classified as industrialised countries. Norway ratified the Kyoto Protocol in 2002 and has an emission obligation related to the first commitment period, 2008 to 2012. The implementation of the Kyoto Protocol is an important part of Norwegian climate policy. The system of opening up flexible implementation and cooperation with countries allows Norway to take on greater commitments and contribute to greater global reductions than would be possible through national implementation alone. The Christian People's Party has always considered flexible implementation to be a complement to national measures. The red-green government, which was responsible for the whole of the last commitment period, made cooperation and quota trade the main instrument. Norway’s emissions today are still close to 6 pst. above 1990 levels. At the end of the first commitment period of the Kyoto Protocol, 31 December 2012, the government had to carry out significant quota purchases in developing countries in order to meet our national obligations, in addition to the excess of 10 pst. This was because we had not managed to reduce national emissions in line with our commitments. It does not provide credibility. Norway is also in a poor position to achieve the target of reducing emissions of equivalent 30 pst. of Norway’s 1990 emissions by 2020, and in particular the target of taking about two thirds of the emission reductions nationally. There are less than eight years left until 2020, and the emissions curve shows few signs of starting to point downwards. By comparison, our neighbouring countries Denmark and Sweden have managed to reduce their national greenhouse gas emissions by 20 and 16 pst respectively. compared to 1990 levels. In Sweden, what is to be done with the considerable surplus of allowances now held by the country is discussed, after overfilling its international commitments by more than 10 pst. At the Doha United Nations Climate Change Conference in December 2012, amendments to the Kyoto Protocol establishing emission obligations for the period 2013-2020 were adopted. For Norway, the decision involves a new emission obligation, which is in line with the objective of reducing emissions by 30 pst. in 2020 compared to the 1990s. The new commitment implies that Norway’s greenhouse gas emissions over the period 2013-2020 have to be on average 16 pst. lower than Norway’s 1990 emissions. I welcome the broad consensus in the position that the government should involve the Stortinget in a discussion on the alignment of Norway’s international climate action, and that there should be a case for the Stortinget on ‘setting up Norway’s international climate action during the spring 2014 session’.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e4153	The most pleasant thing about this case is that it is a unanimous increase in favour of the Kyoto 2 agreement, and that this is the basis for the commitments we make towards 2020. But then the real job begins: what does it mean for Norway to sign the Kyoto 2 agreement? With the Kyoto agreement of 1997, we got a commitment that it would only be 1 pst. increase by 2012. We have a larger increase, 6-7 pst., of CO2 emissions. What enables us to keep the Kyoto agreement is that it allows for a flexible implementation in terms of the purchase of allowances. But it is important – as the representative of the Christian People's Party also pointed out – that this should be a supplement rather than a general rule. So far, there has been the general rule and the way to resolve commitments under the Kyoto Agreement. From 2013 to 2020, emissions shall average — according to the Kyoto 2 commitments — down 16 pst. towards 2020. Here, it is absolutely essential that this means that Norway is also being cut in a large way. In the climate agreement, the concrete cut obligation is approx. 5 million tonnes, i.e. 10 pst. reduction. Now, when we are going to strengthen the climate agreement, which is widely agreed, also in addition to the cooperation agreement that the government has with the Left and Christian People's Party, it must result in a goal and in measures that are proportionate to the objective, so that quota purchases can be what they were supposed to be, just a supplement. It is important to take into account that this is not a perfect agreement at all. With the Kyoto 2 agreement, at least we do not reach a reduction of 85 pst. towards 2050, which is necessary to achieve the two-degree target. However, the agreement is a necessary step in order to move forward also in the international climate negotiations. And just to move on, it's important for Norway to show that we're not just willing to use that wealth, that bag of money that we have to buy quotas internationally. We also need to show willingness to drive both technological development and concrete emissions cuts in Norway. Therefore, I am pleased that Stortinget is unanimous in this matter. A much more important proposal for a decision is the proposal ‘to ask the government to come back to the Stortingen with a case of setting up Norway’s international climate action during the spring 2014 session’. It is in this case that the Stortinget must outsource its real will to implement new climate action. It is in this matter that we must have a majority in which we specifically strengthen the climate agreement. The climate agreement, with a reduction of 5 million tonnes by 2020, requires significantly greater efforts than today. If we are to strengthen this under the Kyoto 2 agreement, it requires even more. I think it is wrong to rely on the action that really seems the worst. We need targets for both energy efficiency improvement and renewable energy. We're not just going to push forward a reduction, we're going to push forward a necessary technological development, which we need if we're going to achieve the two-degree goal. We are not just going to go with results in Europe and Norway, we are also going to go with measures in the rest of the world. Then we have to have ambitious goals.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e4215	Climate change is in progress. We need to increase the global total effort if we are to achieve the goal of limiting heating to two degrees. The emission reductions currently implemented or planned are not sufficient. According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, global emissions must reach their peak by 2020. We need reinforced efforts both in the short and long term. A new commitment period under the Kyoto Protocol is important for several reasons. Firstly, the Kyoto Protocol is the only legally binding agreement in the field of climate change that sets numerical emission obligations for countries. Secondly, the Kyoto Protocol has a robust system of common rules on how countries deal with their climate commitments. This is something Norway will also work for in a new climate agreement. Thirdly, the Kyoto Protocol also has a UN-approved allowance market for cost-effective implementation. This means that countries can contribute to major emission reductions globally. The possibility of quota buying and cooperation with other countries allows Norway to contribute to major global reductions. This is one of the objectives of international agreements — to help countries do more together than that country can do alone. A new commitment period will mean numerical emission obligations for several countries until 2020 and the continuation of the good systems established under the Kyoto Protocol, which are an important part of Norwegian climate policy. A new commitment period was also a prerequisite for agreeing on the mandate for negotiating a new, wider climate agreement to apply from 2020. It is therefore an important step towards broader climate cooperation under the UNFCCC. It is a crossroads that no more countries have committed themselves to the second commitment period of the Kyoto Protocol. It is all the more important for confidence in the negotiations that countries take the lead and follow up the political decisions taken on enhanced action in the field of climate change. I am therefore pleased that the Committee recommends that the Storting agree to approve the amendments to the Kyoto Protocol. For the post-2020 period, Norway is working for a broad, ambitious and binding agreement in line with the two-degree objective. In Warsaw, a Roadmap was decided where all countries will make the homework to prepare for the new climate agreement, to be adopted in 2015. Norway also has an important homework to do here, which I know the committee has noticed. Therefore, 2014 will be an important year in building up until 2015. Several political meetings have been agreed in 2014 to discuss increased ambitions and to create a political pressure until 2015. A Ministerial Round Table has been agreed to discuss increased ambitions under the Kyoto Protocol. A ministerial meeting has also been agreed to discuss increased ambitions more broadly by 2020. This is part of the follow-up to the work programme on increased ambitions under the UNFCCC. It has also been agreed to a meeting of world leaders in September 2014 to which the UN Secretary-General has invited. That meeting is important for mobilising political will until 2015. So the coming year will be important for the international climate work, and I have noticed the British Government's wish that the government return to the Stortinget with a matter of setting up Norway's international climate action during the spring 2014 session. Of course, the government will follow up on that. My response in the previous debate to Heikki Holmås was more general about the climate communication – whether there should be a message or an explanation. Here I have understood that this is a matter. So to the question that was also asked about the letter that is now about the EU Green Paper. The government was in the Grand Council of Europe. The Committee on Energy and the Environment was also invited when the Minister for Oil and Energy, the Minister for Europe and myself were there on 4 November this year. The dialogue has been with the European Committee of the Biggest, which is the body of the Biggest, for dialogue with the government in this area.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e4402	I would also draw upon myself to follow up just a little bit of the content debate that Nikolai Astrup brought up, which is an important debate, and as I think it is important to get wide and specifically discussed, because the content of this is absolutely crucial for this policy and for the ability to put a proper line forward. I must admit that if one looks at the energy policy in the EU and at what things have driven change, what has driven the green shift within parts of the EU, but which has also affected the world in a proper way, then there is no doubt that the Germans' targeted investment in renewable energy, the Germans' targeted investment in solar cells and solar panels, have helped to drive forward the transformation that is now making solar cells competitive with fossil energy sources in a very large proportion of the world's markets. This has not been driven by CO2 prices. Twice during the period of operation of the EU ETS, the CO2 market has collapsed. First of all, it was due to recessions in the EU, which have led to much, much lower demand than it would otherwise have been – not because there has been a violent change and upturn, but simply because of the fact that there have been downturns. In other words, it has not helped to drive forward the green shift as we know it is absolutely necessary that we get to. Why? Well, if you're an investor and you're wondering if you're going to invest in a technology that allows you to save a lot of CO2 emissions in the future, it's crucial for you to look at what the cost path will be like in the future – how much money you're actually saving. If the price fluctuates as much as it has done in the EU quota market today, and it is down to around EUR 3.50, in other words not too much money, then that is not enough. It's a disincentive for investors. An allowance price, an obligation to invest in renewable energy, a clear price that is fixed – all there are different incentives that have worked better than one general incentive – or one general system, quota price. That is why I fully agree with Ola Elvestuen that the recording we should have given to the EU was to go for more goals – not one goal, but several goals. That is why I believe it is important that we get a anchoring process in the Storting for this, which is the crucial question of how we prepare for the matter that the Minister for Climate and Environment is going to present in the spring, where our objectives and our input to the climate summit in September are to be. That's why it's important to get a proper discussion and anchoring of it.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e4422	The representative Nikolai Astrup has spoken twice in the past and is given the floor to a brief comment, limited to 1 minute.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-12-12.ana.ud421e4425	If the representative Eidsvoll Holmås had taken the trouble to attend the European Committee meeting on 4 November, he could convey these views to the Councils. I understand that the representatives Solhjell and Eidsvoll Holmås are dissatisfied that the government has a different view than them, but that does not mean that Stortinget has not been consulted. It just means that the government has a different view. As far as the use of instruments is concerned, let me just say this: yes, the evolution of solar photovoltaic prices has been important for the world, but the emissions in Germany are rising, and that is despite all the emissions we have to measure, and then there are many different instruments to bring down emissions. It could be renewable energy, and it could be energy efficiency improvement. But the most important thing to measure is whether we're actually able to reduce emissions. That is what the government has now agreed on; that it should have a target of emission reductions, and then, of course, different instruments should be used to achieve that goal. But it also has to be up to national states to decide how to achieve targets.
